\draw[DistGold,thick,dashed]
  (0.3,0) to[out=-45,in=182] (6.5,-1.1);
\node[font=\tiny\sffamily\color{DistRed}] at (5.3,1.9)
  {extractive};
\node[font=\tiny\sffamily\color{DistRed}] at (5.3,-1.9)
  {extractive};
\filldraw[DistBlue] (0.3,0) circle (2pt)
  node[above left,font=\small\sffamily\color{DistBlue}]{$x$};
\node[font=\small\sffamily\color{DistTeal}] at (3.2,2.1)
  {$\reach(x,t)$};
\node[font=\small\sffamily\color{DistGold}] at (3.1,0)
  {$\adm(x,t)$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The admissibility manifold $\adm(x,t)$ (gold) is a
  proper subset of the reachability set (teal). States in
  the reachability set but outside the admissibility
  manifold are \emph{extractive}: reaching them collapses
  future possibility below threshold $\alpha$.}
\label{fig:adm-manifold}
\end{figure}

\section{Admissibility Existence}
\label{sec:ch14-existence}

\begin{theorem}{Admissibility Existence Theorem}{adm-exist}
  Let $(X,\Phi)$ be a compact dynamical system with
  continuous $F(\cdot,t,\tau)$ for all $t,\tau>0$. Then for
  any $\alpha\in(0,1)$ and $\tau>0$, the admissibility
  manifold $\adm(x,t)$ is non-empty whenever $\reach(x,t)$
  is non-empty and $V_R(x,t)>0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Since $F(y,t,\tau)$ is continuous on the compact set
  $\reach(x,t)$, it attains its maximum at some
  $y^*\in\reach(x,t)$. Let $V^* = F(y^*,t,\tau)$. We need
  $V^* \ge \alpha\cdot V_R(x,t_0,t)$. At minimum, the
  trivial continuation $\gamma(s)=y^*$ for
  $s\in[t,t+\tau]$ gives $F(y^*,t,\tau)\ge0$. Since
  $\reach(x,t)$ is non-empty and compact, and $F$ is
  continuous, the supremum is attained. If $V_R(x,t)>0$
  then $\reach(x,t)$ contains states with non-trivial
  forward dynamics, so $V^*>0$. Choosing
  $\alpha < V^*/V_R(x,t_0,t)$ guarantees
  $\adm(x,t)\ne\emptyset$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{Non-triviality of the threshold}{adm-threshold-rmk}
  The Admissibility Existence Theorem guarantees that
  $\adm$ is non-empty but does not guarantee it contains
  trajectories that are practically useful. For large
  $\alpha$ (requiring the future to be nearly as open as
  the present), the admissibility manifold may be very
  small. The choice of $\alpha$ is a design parameter
  reflecting how strongly a system values future openness
  versus present gain. Chapter~\ref{ch:geometry-futures}
  discusses how this parameter connects to generative
  versus extractive trajectories.
\end{remark}

\section{Observational and Interventional Admissibility}
\label{sec:ch14-obs-int}

A fundamental distinction in causal reasoning separates
\emph{observing} a system from \emph{intervening} on it
\parencite{pearl2000causality}. The same distinction
applies to admissibility.

\begin{definition}{Observational Admissibility}{obs-adm}
  A trajectory $\gamma$ is \emph{observationally admissible}
  if $\gamma(t)\in\adm(x,t)$ for all $t$, where $\adm$ is
  defined relative to the natural dynamics $\Phi$ without
  external intervention.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}{Interventional Admissibility}{int-adm}
  A trajectory $\gamma$ is \emph{interventionally
  admissible} if $\gamma(t)\in\adm^\mathrm{do}(x,t)$ for
  all $t$, where $\adm^\mathrm{do}$ is defined relative to
  the post-intervention dynamics $\Phi^\mathrm{do}$ obtained
  by fixing some variables by external control.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Observational--Interventional Separation}%
  {obs-int-sep}
  Observational and interventional admissibility manifolds
  are generically distinct: $\adm(x,t) \ne \adm^\mathrm{do}(x,t)$.
  Intervention can either expand or contract the
  admissibility manifold relative to observation.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Consider a system with hidden confounders. Under
  observation, trajectories that appear to reach high-$F$
  states may owe their reachability to confounders that are
  not preserved under intervention. Let $Z$ be a confounder
  with $P(Y=y\mid X=x,Z=z) \ne P(Y=y\mid X=x)$. Then
  $\reach(x,t)$ computed from observational data includes
  states reachable only when $Z$ takes specific values.
  After intervention $\mathrm{do}(X=x)$, confounders are
  severed, so $\reach^\mathrm{do}(x,t) \ne \reach(x,t)$ in
  general. Since $\adm$ is defined over $\reach$, they
  differ. For expansion: intervention can remove constraints
  that were present under natural dynamics (e.g.\ medical
  treatment removes disease constraints). For contraction:
  intervention can introduce new constraints (e.g.\ fixing
  a variable eliminates trajectories that depended on its
  variation).
\end{proof}

\begin{example}{Drug Treatment and Admissibility}{drug-adm}
  \bioicon
  Consider a patient's physiological state space.
  Observationally, a patient with untreated hypertension
  follows trajectories that are observationally
  inadmissible: they lead to states (stroke, organ failure)
  from which reachability volume is severely reduced.
  Interventional admissibility under antihypertensive
  treatment expands $\adm^\mathrm{do}$ by removing the
  hypertensive constraint, opening trajectories toward
  states with higher $F$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}{AGI Alignment and Admissibility}{agi-adm}
  \csicon
  An AI system maximising a reward function may follow
  observationally reachable trajectories that are
  interventionally inadmissible: they consume resources or
  close options in ways that reduce future human
  reachability volume. Alignment can be formulated as the
  requirement that the system's trajectories remain within
  the intersection $\adm(x,t)\cap\adm^\mathrm{do}(x,t)$.
\end{example}

\section{Admissibility Distortion}
\label{sec:ch14-distortion}

When a system operates under a compressed or approximate
representation of state space, its estimate of
admissibility may differ systematically from true
admissibility. This is admissibility distortion.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Distortion}{adm-distortion-def}
  Let $\pi:X\to Y$ be a projection (lossy representation).
  The \emph{admissibility distortion} induced by $\pi$ is
  \[
    \Delta_\adm(\pi)
    = \sup_{x\in X} |V_R(x,t) - V_R(\pi(x),t)|,
  \]
  the maximum discrepancy between true reachability volume
  and the volume estimated from the compressed
  representation $\pi(x)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Admissibility Distortion Theorem}{adm-distortion}
  For any non-injective projection $\pi:X\to Y$,
  $\Delta_\adm(\pi) > 0$: every lossy representation
  introduces positive admissibility distortion.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Since $\pi$ is non-injective, there exist
  $x_1,x_2\in X$ with $x_1\ne x_2$ but
  $\pi(x_1)=\pi(x_2)$. Their reachability sets may differ:
  $\reach(x_1,t)\ne\reach(x_2,t)$ in general, since
  reachability depends on the full state, not its
  projection. Under the compressed representation, both
  $x_1$ and $x_2$ are assigned the same estimated
  reachability volume $V_R(\pi(x_1),t)=V_R(\pi(x_2),t)$.
  Hence at least one of these estimates is wrong, giving
  $\Delta_\adm(\pi)>0$.
\end{proof}

Admissibility distortion is the geometric analogue of
projection loss (Chapter~\ref{ch:distinction}) applied to
future possibility rather than present information. Just
as every observation loses present distinctions, every
compressed model loses future distinctions.

\section{The Projection--Admissibility Gap}
\label{sec:ch14-gap}

Admissibility distortion has a particularly important
consequence for systems that reason about their own futures
using compressed self-models.

\begin{definition}{Self-Model}{self-model-ch14}
  A \emph{self-model} of a system with state space $X$ is a
  projection $\hat\pi:X\to\hat{X}$ where $\hat{X}$ is a
  simplified representation of $X$ used by the system
  itself for planning and prediction.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Projection--Admissibility Gap Theorem}%
  {proj-adm-gap}
  Let $\hat\pi:X\to\hat{X}$ be a self-model with compression
  ratio $r = |X|/|\hat{X}| > 1$. Then the system
  underestimates admissibility distortion by at least a
  factor of $\log r$:
  \[
    \Delta_\adm(\hat\pi)
    \ge \frac{\log r}{\log|X|}\cdot V_R(x_\mathrm{max},t).
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Compression by ratio $r$ collapses on average $r$ states
  of $X$ into each state of $\hat{X}$. The reachability
  volume estimated from $\hat\pi(x)$ is therefore an
  average over $r$ distinct true reachability volumes. By
  Jensen's inequality applied to the concave logarithm,
  this average underestimates the maximum reachability
  volume by at least $\log r/\log|X|$ as a fraction of the
  total reachability.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{Self-Model Humility}{self-model-humility}
  The Projection--Admissibility Gap Theorem is a formal
  statement of self-model humility: any system reasoning
  about its own futures from a compressed self-representation
  will systematically underestimate how much admissibility
  it is destroying by its choices. This applies equally to
  biological organisms, institutions, and artificial
  intelligence systems. The more compressed the self-model,
  the larger the gap.
\end{remark}

\section{Admissibility and Repair}
\label{sec:ch14-repair}

The connection between admissibility and the repair
framework of Part~III is direct.

\begin{proposition}{Repair Preserves Admissibility}{repair-adm}
  A repair operation $\repair$ is admissible if and only if
  $V_R(\repair(y),t) \ge V_R(y,t)$: admissible repair does
  not reduce reachability volume.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  By definition, repair restores a distinction to a state
  that is closer to the admissibility manifold. If the
  repair is itself inadmissible --- if it reaches a state
  of lower reachability volume --- then it has consumed
  future possibility in the act of restoring present
  distinction. Such a repair is incoherent with the purpose
  of repair: it removes one damage while introducing a
  structural deterioration of the future. Therefore
  admissible repair satisfies
  $V_R(\repair(y),t)\ge V_R(y,t)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}{Antibiotic Resistance and Inadmissible Repair}%
  {abx-resist}
  \bioicon
  A bacterial population under antibiotic pressure may
  ``repair'' its survival problem through resistance
  mutations. This is observationally successful repair: the
  population survives. But at the population level,
  indiscriminate antibiotic use drives resistance evolution,
  which contracts the admissibility manifold for future
  treatment: fewer antibiotics remain effective. The
  short-term repair is interventionally inadmissible: it
  reduces future reachability volume for the health system.
\end{example}

\section{Admissibility Metrics}
\label{sec:ch14-metrics}

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Score}{adm-score}
  The \emph{admissibility score} of a trajectory
  $\gamma:[t_0,T]\to X$ is
  \[
    \mathcal{A}(\gamma)
    = \frac{1}{T-t_0} \int_{t_0}^T
      \frac{F(\gamma(t),t,\tau)}{V_R(\gamma(t_0),t_0,T)}\,dt,
  \]
  the time-averaged fraction of original reachability
  volume preserved along $\gamma$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Distance}{adm-distance}
  The \emph{admissibility distance} from a state $y$ to the
  admissibility manifold is
  $d_\adm(y) = \inf_{z\in\adm(x,t)} d(y,z)$.
\end{definition}

These metrics provide practical tools for evaluating
whether a given system trajectory is approaching or
receding from admissibility. Chapter~\ref{ch:geometry-futures}
uses them to characterise generative, extractive, and
regenerative trajectories geometrically.

\section{Admissibility in Computation}
\label{sec:ch14-computation}

\csicon

\begin{definition}{Computationally Admissible Program}{comp-adm}
  A program $P$ is \emph{computationally admissible} from
  configuration $c_0$ if at every step $t$, the set of
  configurations reachable in the next $\tau$ steps
  satisfies
  \[
    \frac{V_R^P(c_t,\tau)}{V_R^P(c_0,\tau)} \ge \alpha,
  \]
  ruling out execution paths that close off large fractions
  of the future configuration space.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}{Reversible Computing}{rev-comp}
  Reversible computing maintains computational admissibility
  exactly: every operation is a bijection on configurations,
  so $V_R$ is constant and $\mathcal{A}(\gamma)=1$.
  Irreversible operations (such as erasing a bit) are
  inadmissible in the strict sense: they collapse
  computational reachability. Landauer's principle
  \parencite{landauer1961irreversibility} can be reread as
  the thermodynamic cost of computational inadmissibility.
\end{example}

\section{The Admissibility Manifold as a Sheaf}
\label{sec:ch14-sheaf}

For readers with background in category theory or algebraic
topology, the admissibility manifold has a natural
sheaf-theoretic interpretation that connects to the
Distinguishability Geometry programme.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Sheaf}{adm-sheaf}
  Let $\mathcal{T}$ be the topology on
  $X\times\mathbb{R}_{\ge0}$. The \emph{admissibility sheaf}
  $\mathcal{A}$ assigns to each open set $U\in\mathcal{T}$
  the set of locally admissible trajectories,
  \[
    \mathcal{A}(U)
    = \{\gamma : U \to X : \gamma \text{ is admissible on } U\},
  \]
  with restriction maps given by restricting trajectories
  to smaller open sets.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}{Admissibility Sheaf is a Sheaf}{adm-sheaf-prop}
  $\mathcal{A}$ satisfies the sheaf axioms: identity,
  locality, and gluing.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  \emph{Identity}: the empty trajectory is admissible on
  the empty set. \emph{Locality}: if $\gamma$ is admissible
  on each $U_i$ in an open cover, then it is admissible on
  $U$. \emph{Gluing}: if $\gamma_i\in\mathcal{A}(U_i)$
  agree on overlaps $U_i\cap U_j$, their union defines an
  admissible trajectory on $\bigcup_i U_i$, provided the
  gluing is consistent with dynamics.
\end{proof}

The sheaf structure means admissibility is a genuinely
local-to-global property: global admissibility is
determined by local admissibility conditions. This has
direct implications for distributed systems, where no
single node has global knowledge but local admissibility
conditions can nonetheless guarantee global future
preservation.

\section*{Chapter Summary}
\begin{chapsummary}
  \item Reachability is necessary but not sufficient:
        admissibility asks which reachable states preserve
        future reachability.
  \item The admissibility manifold $\adm(x,t)$ is the
        closed set of states from which reachability volume
        remains above threshold $\alpha$
        (\cref{defn:adm-manifold}).
  \item $\adm$ is non-empty whenever reachability is
        non-empty and the dynamics are continuous on a
        compact space (\cref{thm:adm-exist}).
  \item Observational and interventional admissibility are
        generically distinct; interventions can expand or
        contract $\adm$ (\cref{thm:obs-int-sep}).
  \item Every lossy representation induces positive
        admissibility distortion (\cref{thm:adm-distortion}).
  \item Compressed self-models systematically underestimate
        the admissibility they destroy
        (\cref{thm:proj-adm-gap}).
  \item Admissible repair does not reduce reachability
        volume (\cref{prop:repair-adm}).
  \item Reversible computing is maximally admissible;
        irreversible operations are inadmissible at cost
        equal to Landauer's bound.
  \item The admissibility manifold forms a sheaf, making
        admissibility a local-to-global property.
\end{chapsummary}

\subsection*{Exercises}

\begin{exercise}[CS]
  A garbage-collected programming language periodically
  frees unreachable memory. Model this as an admissibility
  operation. What is being repaired? Is the repair
  admissible in the sense of \cref{prop:repair-adm}? What
  would an \emph{inadmissible} memory operation look like?
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Bio]
  Model cell senescence as an admissibility collapse event.
  Identify the state space $X$, the admissibility threshold
  $\alpha$, and the mechanism by which senescence reduces
  $V_R$. What would an admissibility-preserving alternative
  to senescence look like?
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Prove that if $\adm(x,t)=\reach(x,t)$ for all $t$, then
  the system is reversible. (Hint: use the definition of
  admissibility threshold and the Future Cone Theorem,
  Chapter~\ref{ch:reachability}.)
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[CS]
  Define an \emph{admissibility-preserving compiler
  optimisation} as one that does not reduce the set of
  possible program behaviours. Give an example of an
  optimisation that is admissible and one that is not.
  (Consider inlining, dead code elimination, and
  speculative execution.)
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Let $\pi:X\to Y$ be a projection with compression ratio
  $r=2$ (every two states mapped to one). Compute the
  minimum admissibility distortion $\Delta_\adm(\pi)$ as a
  function of $V_R(x_\mathrm{max},t)$. How does the
  distortion scale as $r\to\infty$?
\end{exercise}

% ---- Chapter 15 --------------------------------------------
\chapter{The Geometry of Admissible Futures}
\label{ch:geometry-futures}
\begin{epigraph}{What we call the beginning is often the end.
  And to make an end is to make a beginning.}%
  {T.S.\ Eliot, \textit{Little Gidding}}
\end{epigraph}

\begin{definition}{Full Admissibility Invariant}{full-adm-inv}
  $\mathbf{I}_\adm(x,t)
  =(\Vol(\adm),S_\adm,\chi(\adm),\kappa_\adm)$:
  volume, diversity, topological complexity, and curvature.
  \index{admissibility!invariant (full)|textbf}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}{Uncompensated Entropy Growth}{uncomp-entropy}
  Entropy growth is \emph{uncompensated} if not accompanied
  by repair, adaptation, or distinction generation:
  $\Delta S>0$ and $\Delta D\le0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Future Volume Theorem}{future-vol}
  (i)~$\Vol(\adm(t))\ge0$ is well-defined.
  (ii)~$\Vol(\adm(t))\le V_R(x,t)$.
  (iii)~Under uncompensated entropy growth,
  $\Vol(\adm(t))\le\Vol(\adm(t_0))$.
  (iv)~Under regenerative dynamics,
  $\Vol(\adm(t))\ge\Vol(\adm(t_0))$.
  (v)~Under distinction-generating dynamics,
  $\Vol(\adm(t))$ is strictly increasing.
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}{Trajectory Classification}{traj-classes}
  $\gamma$ is \textbf{generative} if
  $\frac{d}{dt}\Vol(\adm)\ge0$;
  \textbf{extractive} if $<0$;
  \textbf{regenerative} if generative and
  $\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\Vol(\adm)\ge0$;
  \textbf{pathologically continuing} if extractive but
  consuming distinction structures to avoid termination.
  \index{trajectory!generative|textbf}
  \index{trajectory!extractive|textbf}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Future Bottleneck Theorem}{bottleneck}
  For extractive $\gamma$ with $\Vol(\adm)>0$ on $[t_0,T]$
  and repair continuously exercised at capacity
  $\kappa_\repair>0$:
  (i)~$\gamma$ passes through a bottleneck $t^*$;
  (ii)~$\min_t\Vol(\adm(\gamma(t),t))\ge\kappa_\repair$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}{Future Preservation Theorem}{future-preserve}
  Let $\gamma_1$ be generative and $\gamma_2$ extractive
  from the same $x$, with equal immediate reward and $r$
  non-decreasing in $\Vol(\adm)$.
  Then $\exists T^*$ such that $\forall T>T^*$:
  $\int_{t_0}^T r(\gamma_1)\,dt\ge
  \int_{t_0}^T r(\gamma_2)\,dt$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}{Admissibility Curvature Theorem}{adm-curv}
  A perturbation of size $\epsilon$ in a high-curvature
  direction changes $\Vol(\adm)$ by
  $O(\epsilon\cdot\kappa_\adm)$; in a low-curvature
  direction, $O(\epsilon^2)$.
  High-curvature points are decision points where
  trajectory choices have disproportionate consequences.
  \index{admissibility!curvature|textbf}
\end{theorem}

\begin{principle}{Generative Admissibility Principle}{gap-ch15}
  A trajectory is valuable insofar as it preserves the
  capacity for future distinction-production.
  \index{generative admissibility!principle|textbf}
\end{principle}

\begin{theorem}{Future Distinction Optimality Theorem}%
  {gat-ch15}
  Among trajectories achieving equal reward $R$, with
  $r$ continuous and non-decreasing in $\Vol(\adm)$,
  generatively admissible trajectories maximise
  future distinction-producing capacity:
  $\mathbf{I}_\adm(\gamma^*(T),T)
  \ge_\mathrm{lex}\mathbf{I}_\adm(\gamma(T),T)$
  for all $\gamma\in\Gamma_R^-$.
  \index{generative admissibility!theorem|textbf}
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}{Intelligence and Admissibility}{gat-intelligence}
  A system is intelligent iff its trajectories are
  generatively admissible w.r.t.\ its ecology.
\end{corollary}
\begin{corollary}{Science and Admissibility}{gat-science}
  A scientific tradition is generatively admissible insofar
  as it expands the volume, entropy, and topological
  complexity of future admissible questions.
\end{corollary}
\begin{corollary}{Governance and Admissibility}{gat-governance}
  Governance is generatively admissible insofar as it
  preserves the reachability volume and diversity of
  policy options for future generations.
\end{corollary}
\begin{corollary}{Memory and Admissibility}{gat-memory}
  Memory is generatively admissible insofar as it
  preserves recoverability needed for future repair.
\end{corollary}
\begin{corollary}{Repair and Admissibility}{gat-repair}
  Admissible repair is generatively admissible;
  repair reducing $\Vol(\adm)$ is worse than no repair.
\end{corollary}
\begin{corollary}{Cosmological Renewal}{gat-cosmo}
  Expyrotic renewal (Ch.\,\ref{ch:expyrotic}) is
  generatively admissible: it restores the universe's
  distinction-producing capacity.
\end{corollary}

\section{From Geometry to Ethics}

The entire progression has been mathematical. No step has
been normative by assumption. Yet the Future Distinction
Optimality Theorem establishes that agents ignoring
admissible volume are \emph{eventually dominated} by those
that do not. Strategic dominance is derived, not postulated.
Moral preference, if it exists, is a further claim.
Strategic preference is already a theorem. The framework
crosses from geometry to ethics not by assumption but
by proof.

\section*{Chapter Summary}
\begin{chapsummary}
  \item The full invariant
        $\mathbf{I}_\adm=(\Vol,S_\adm,\chi,\kappa_\adm)$
        captures volume, diversity, topology, and curvature.
  \item The Future Volume Theorem holds under uncompensated
        entropy growth; evolution and learning are not
        counterexamples.
  \item Growth is not generativity.
  \item The Bottleneck Theorem requires repair to be
        \emph{exercised} (\cref{thm:bottleneck}).
  \item Generative trajectories dominate extractive ones
        over long horizons (\cref{thm:future-preserve}).
  \item The Future Distinction Optimality Theorem proves
        the dominance of generative trajectories
        (\cref{thm:gat-ch15}).
\end{chapsummary}

\part{Physical Realizations}

% ============================================================
%  CHAPTERS 16–19: PHYSICAL REALIZATIONS
%  Part VI: Physical Realizations
%  The Ecology of Distinctions — Flyxion
% ============================================================

% ============================================================
%  CHAPTER 16: RSVP — THE SCALAR-VECTOR PLENUM
% ============================================================
\chapter{RSVP: The Scalar--Vector Plenum}
\label{ch:rsvp}

\begin{epigraph}{%
  A physical theory is not a list of forces. It is a
  statement of what can be stored, what can move, and
  what can be lost.}%
  {Author}
\end{epigraph}

\begin{objectives}
  \item Show that the RSVP fields $(\Phi,\mathbf{v},S)$ emerge
        from the distinction-repair-reachability framework
        rather than being postulated independently.
  \item Prove the Three-Field Necessity Theorem.
  \item Derive the RSVP continuity and constraint-accumulation
        equations.
  \item Prove the Reachability Capacity Theorem connecting
        RSVP directly to Part~V.
  \item Derive lamphrodyne relaxation and the RSVP
        Admissibility Theorem.
  \item Prove the Physical Realization Theorem.
\end{objectives}

\section{Three Quantities, Not One}
\label{sec:ch16-three}

The purpose of this chapter is not to re-derive the entire
RSVP programme from scratch. It is to supply the minimum
mathematical machinery required to connect
Parts~I--V to a physical realization.

The central claim is that a physical system capable of
representing distinction-theoretic structure requires
exactly three independent geometric quantities:
\emph{capacity}, \emph{transport}, and \emph{constraint}.
These become, respectively, the scalar field $\Phi$, the
vector field $\mathbf{v}$, and the constraint field $S$ —
together, the \emph{Relativistic Scalar--Vector Plenum}
(\gls{rsvp}).

\begin{definition}{RSVP State Variables}{rsvp-fields}
  An RSVP system is described by the triple
  \[
    (\Phi, \mathbf{v}, S),
  \]
  where $\Phi(x,t)$ is the \emph{scalar capacity field},
  $\mathbf{v}(x,t)$ is the \emph{transport velocity field},
  and $S(x,t)$ is the \emph{constraint density field}.
  \index{RSVP (Relativistic Scalar Vector Plenum)|textbf}
\end{definition}

The interpretation of each field connects directly to the
preceding fifteen chapters rather than to independent
physical postulates.

\begin{definition}{Capacity Field}{capacity-field}
  The capacity field $\Phi$ measures the local ability of
  a region to support future distinctions.
  \index{RSVP!capacity field}
\end{definition}

Thus $\Phi$ is not energy, not matter, and not information
in the Shannon sense. It is available
distinction-producing capacity, in the sense developed
throughout Chapters~13--15.

\begin{definition}{Constraint Field}{constraint-field}
  The constraint field $S(x,t)$ measures accumulated
  restriction on future reachability volume.
  \index{RSVP!constraint field}
\end{definition}

Entropy, on this reading, becomes geometrized: $S$ is not
a count of microstates but a local field whose growth
restricts the admissible future.

\begin{definition}{Transport Field}{transport-field}
  The transport field $\mathbf{v}(x,t)$ describes the
  propagation of distinction-producing capacity through
  the domain.
  \index{RSVP!transport field}
\end{definition}

\section{The Three-Field Necessity Theorem}
\label{sec:ch16-necessity}

\begin{theorem}{Three-Field Necessity Theorem}{three-field-necessity}
  Any physical theory capable of representing
  \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
    \item stored distinction capacity,
    \item movement of distinction capacity, and
    \item restriction of distinction capacity
  \end{enumerate}
  requires at least one scalar field, one vector field,
  and one constraint field.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Stored capacity requires a scalar magnitude at each
  point, since storage is a quantity without intrinsic
  direction. Transport requires directional information
  in addition to magnitude, hence a vector field. Constraint
  accumulation requires a quantity that independently
  measures admissible-volume reduction: it cannot be
  recovered from $\Phi$ and $\mathbf{v}$ alone, since a
  system may transport capacity without losing future
  reachability, or lose reachability without any change in
  stored capacity. No two of the three quantities determine
  the third. Therefore all three are independent and all
  three are necessary.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{Why this theorem matters}{three-field-remark}
  This theorem is significant because it makes the RSVP
  triple appear inevitable rather than invented. Any
  framework satisfying the requirements of
  Chapters~1--15 --- storage, transport, and restriction of
  distinction capacity --- is forced into this three-field
  structure, independently of any commitment to a
  particular physical interpretation.
\end{remark}

\section{Capacity Conservation}
\label{sec:ch16-conservation}

\begin{theorem}{Capacity Conservation Theorem}{capacity-conservation}
  For any region $\Omega$, capacity evolves according to
  \[
    \frac{\partial \Phi}{\partial t}
    + \nabla\cdot(\Phi\mathbf{v})
    = Q - R,
  \]
  where $Q$ creates distinction capacity and $R$ destroys it.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Apply conservation to an arbitrary volume $\Omega$: the
  rate of change of capacity within $\Omega$ equals inflow
  across $\partial\Omega$ plus internal sources minus
  internal sinks. Applying the divergence theorem to convert
  the boundary flux into a volume integral of
  $\nabla\cdot(\Phi\mathbf{v})$ yields
  \[
    \frac{\partial \Phi}{\partial t}
    + \nabla\cdot(\Phi\mathbf{v})
    = Q - R. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

This is the fundamental RSVP continuity equation, governing
all subsequent dynamics in Part~VI.

\begin{definition}{Constraint Potential}{constraint-potential}
  Define the \emph{constraint potential}
  \[
    U_S = \int_\Omega S \, dV.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Constraint Accumulation Theorem}{constraint-accumulation-rsvp}
  Constraint density evolves according to
  \[
    \frac{\partial S}{\partial t}
    = \alpha D - \beta R + \gamma \nabla^2 S,
  \]
  where $D$ is the local rate of distinction destruction.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By the Distinction--Entropy Duality
  (\cref{law:entropy-dual}), destroyed distinctions
  contribute positively to hidden multiplicity, here
  represented by $\alpha D$. Repair reduces constraint at
  rate $\beta R$, by the Repair--Entropy Theorem
  (\cref{thm:repair-entropy}). The diffusive term
  $\gamma\nabla^2 S$ represents the spatial spreading of
  constraint through the medium, consistent with the
  Second Law of Distinction Dynamics
  (\cref{law:second-law-dist}).
\end{proof}

\section{The Reachability Bridge}
\label{sec:ch16-bridge}

The next theorem is the central bridge between RSVP and the
geometric machinery of Part~V.

\begin{theorem}{Reachability Capacity Theorem}{rsvp-reachability}
  Reachability volume satisfies
  \[
    V_R(x,t) \propto \Phi(x,t)\, e^{-S(x,t)}.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Increasing capacity $\Phi$ enlarges the set of reachable
  futures: more stored distinction-producing capacity
  permits more admissible trajectories. Increasing
  constraint $S$ reduces reachable futures, by the
  Constraint Volume Theorem (\cref{thm:constraint-vol}).
  By Chapter~13, $V_R$ must therefore increase monotonically
  with $\Phi$ and decrease monotonically with $S$. The
  simplest invariant satisfying both monotonicity
  conditions simultaneously is the multiplicative form
  \[
    V_R = K\, \Phi\, e^{-S}
  \]
  for some constant $K$, since exponential decay is the
  unique functional form compatible with constraint
  additivity: two independent regions of constraint
  $S_1, S_2$ combine as $S_1 + S_2$, requiring a
  multiplicative reduction $e^{-S_1}e^{-S_2} = e^{-(S_1+S_2)}$
  in reachable volume.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{The most important bridge}{rsvp-bridge-remark}
  This theorem is arguably the single most important
  connection between RSVP and the admissibility programme
  of Part~V. It converts every reachability and
  admissibility result of Chapters~13--15 into a statement
  about the physical fields $\Phi$ and $S$.
\end{remark}

\section{The RSVP Action}
\label{sec:ch16-action}

\begin{definition}{RSVP Action Functional}{rsvp-action}
  \[
    \mathcal{A}
    = \int \left(
        \tfrac{1}{2}\Phi|\mathbf{v}|^2
        - U(\Phi)
        - \lambda S
      \right) dV \, dt.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{RSVP Euler--Lagrange Equations}{rsvp-el}
  Stationary points of $\mathcal{A}$ satisfy
  \[
    \frac{\delta\mathcal{A}}{\delta\Phi} = 0,
    \qquad
    \frac{\delta\mathcal{A}}{\delta\mathbf{v}} = 0,
    \qquad
    \frac{\delta\mathcal{A}}{\delta S} = 0.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
  Standard variational calculus applied to $\mathcal{A}$;
  the detailed component-wise derivation is given in
  Appendix~\ref{app:math}.
\end{proof}

\section{Lamphrodyne Relaxation}
\label{sec:ch16-lamphrodyne}

\begin{definition}{Lamphrodyne Relaxation}{lamphrodyne-relaxation}
  A trajectory obeys \emph{lamphrodyne relaxation} if
  \[
    \frac{d\Phi}{dt}
    = -\mu \frac{\partial S}{\partial \Phi}.
  \]
  \index{lamphrodyne relaxation|textbf}
\end{definition}

This gives the geometric mechanism that drives systems
toward admissible regions: capacity flows so as to reduce
the local marginal constraint.

\begin{theorem}{Lamphrodyne Stability Theorem}{lamphrodyne}
  Along a lamphrodyne trajectory,
  \[
    \frac{dS}{dt} \le 0.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Substituting $\frac{d\Phi}{dt} = -\mu\,\partial S/\partial\Phi$
  into the chain rule,
  \[
    \frac{dS}{dt}
    = \frac{\partial S}{\partial \Phi}\frac{d\Phi}{dt}
    = -\mu \left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial \Phi}\right)^{\!2}
    \le 0. \qedhere
  \]
\end{proof}

This is essentially the RSVP analogue of gradient descent
in constraint space, and it furnishes the dynamical
mechanism underlying gravitational attraction in
Chapter~\ref{ch:gravity}.

\section{RSVP and Admissibility}
\label{sec:ch16-admissibility}

\begin{theorem}{RSVP Admissibility Theorem}{rsvp-admissibility}
  Generatively admissible trajectories satisfy
  \[
    \frac{d}{dt}\bigl(\Phi e^{-S}\bigr) \ge 0.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By the Reachability Capacity Theorem
  (\cref{thm:rsvp-reachability}), $V_R \propto \Phi e^{-S}$.
  By the Generative Admissibility Principle
  (\cref{prc:gap-final}), $\frac{dV_R}{dt} \ge 0$ along
  admissible trajectories. Substitution gives
  $\frac{d}{dt}(\Phi e^{-S}) \ge 0$.
\end{proof}

\section{Physical Realization}
\label{sec:ch16-realization}

\begin{theorem}{Physical Realization Theorem}{rsvp-realization}
  The RSVP variables $(\Phi, \mathbf{v}, S)$ constitute a
  physical realization of the distinction, repair,
  reachability, and admissibility structures developed in
  Chapters~1--15.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  $\Phi$ encodes distinction capacity in the sense of
  \cref{defn:dist-capacity}; $\mathbf{v}$ encodes transport
  of that capacity; $S$ encodes admissible-volume
  restriction in the sense of Chapter~14. By the
  Reachability Capacity Theorem and the RSVP Admissibility
  Theorem, the dynamics of $(\Phi,\mathbf{v},S)$ reproduce
  the reachability and admissibility structure of
  Part~V exactly. Hence every abstract theorem of
  Chapters~1--15 admits a physical instantiation under this
  triple.
\end{proof}

At this point Chapters~17--19 may focus on gravity and
cosmology without re-establishing the entire formal
structure. Chapter~16 is the bridge from the abstract
geometry of admissibility to the concrete dynamics of a
physical universe.

\section{Existence, Stability, and Attractors}
\label{sec:ch16-existence}

The preceding sections established the RSVP field equations
and their connection to reachability and admissibility. What
they have not yet established is whether the coupled system
$(\Phi,\mathbf{v},S)$ actually admits well-behaved solutions,
and whether those solutions settle into recognisable
long-term regimes. This section supplies that missing layer:
a local well-posedness result, an energy functional with a
Lyapunov-type decay property, and a characterisation of RSVP
attractors.

\begin{definition}{RSVP Energy Functional}{rsvp-energy}
  For a domain $\Omega\subseteq X$, define
  \[
    E[\Phi,\mathbf{v},S]
    = \int_\Omega \left(
        \alpha|\nabla\Phi|^2
        + \beta|\mathbf{v}|^2
        + \gamma S^2
      \right) dV,
    \qquad \alpha,\beta,\gamma > 0.
  \]
  \index{RSVP!energy functional|textbf}
\end{definition}

The three terms penalise, respectively, sharp spatial
variation in capacity, kinetic transport, and constraint
magnitude. $E$ is non-negative and vanishes only on the
trivial configuration $\nabla\Phi=\mathbf{v}=S=0$.

\begin{theorem}{Local Well-Posedness Theorem}{rsvp-well-posed}
  Let $\Omega\subseteq X$ be bounded with smooth boundary,
  and let $(\Phi_0,\mathbf{v}_0,S_0)$ be initial data with
  $E[\Phi_0,\mathbf{v}_0,S_0] < \infty$. Then the coupled
  system consisting of the Capacity Conservation Theorem
  (\cref{thm:capacity-conservation}), the Constraint
  Accumulation Theorem
  (\cref{thm:constraint-accumulation-rsvp}), and lamphrodyne
  relaxation (\cref{defn:lamphrodyne-relaxation}) admits a
  unique solution $(\Phi,\mathbf{v},S)$ on some maximal
  interval $[0,T_{\max})$, depending continuously on the
  initial data.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
  The continuity equation for $\Phi$ and the
  reaction--diffusion equation for $S$ are both
  quasilinear parabolic-type equations once
  $\mathbf{v}$ is treated as a given coefficient field;
  standard fixed-point arguments (Banach fixed point on a
  short time interval, using the finiteness of
  $E[\Phi_0,\mathbf{v}_0,S_0]$ to bound the relevant
  Sobolev norms) give local existence and uniqueness for
  $\Phi$ and $S$ given $\mathbf{v}$. Substituting back into
  the Euler--Lagrange equation for $\mathbf{v}$
  (\cref{thm:rsvp-el}) and iterating the fixed point over
  the triple $(\Phi,\mathbf{v},S)$ yields a contraction on a
  sufficiently short time interval, by finiteness of $E$ and
  boundedness of $\Omega$. Continuous dependence on initial
  data follows from a standard Grönwall estimate applied to
  the difference of two solutions.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{Global existence is not claimed}{rsvp-global-caveat}
  The theorem is deliberately local. Whether $T_{\max}=\infty$
  in general --- global well-posedness --- depends on
  whether $E$ remains bounded along the flow, which is
  precisely what the Lyapunov argument below addresses under
  the additional hypothesis of admissibility.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}{Energy Decay Theorem}{rsvp-energy-decay}
  Along any trajectory obeying lamphrodyne relaxation
  (\cref{defn:lamphrodyne-relaxation}) with admissible
  dynamics, the energy functional is non-increasing:
  \[
    \frac{dE}{dt} \le 0.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Differentiating $E$ under the integral sign and applying
  the Capacity Conservation and Constraint Accumulation
  equations gives
  \[
    \frac{dE}{dt}
    = \int_\Omega \left(
        2\alpha\,\nabla\Phi\cdot\nabla\dot\Phi
        + 2\beta\,\mathbf{v}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{v}}
        + 2\gamma S\dot S
      \right) dV.
  \]
  By the Lamphrodyne Stability Theorem
  (\cref{thm:lamphrodyne}), $\dot S$ is driven by
  $-\mu(\partial S/\partial\Phi)^2 \le 0$ along the
  relaxation flow, and the transport term
  $\mathbf{v}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{v}}$ is controlled by the
  dissipative part of the Euler--Lagrange equations
  (\cref{thm:rsvp-el}), which by construction extremises
  $\mathcal{A}$ subject to non-increasing kinetic
  contribution under admissible boundary conditions.
  Integrating by parts on the $\nabla\Phi\cdot\nabla\dot\Phi$
  term and using that boundary flux vanishes for admissible
  trajectories (no capacity is injected at $\partial\Omega$)
  leaves a sum of non-positive terms, giving
  $dE/dt \le 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}{Global Existence Under Bounded Energy}%
  {rsvp-global-exist}
  If $E[\Phi_0,\mathbf{v}_0,S_0] < \infty$ and the
  trajectory remains admissible, then $T_{\max} = \infty$:
  the local solution of \cref{thm:rsvp-well-posed} extends
  globally in time.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  By \cref{thm:rsvp-energy-decay}, $E$ is non-increasing and
  bounded below by $0$, hence bounded on
  $[0,T_{\max})$. Standard continuation criteria for
  quasilinear parabolic systems state that a local solution
  fails to extend only if the relevant energy norm blows up
  in finite time; since $E$ remains bounded, no blow-up
  occurs, and the solution extends to all $t \ge 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}{RSVP Attractor}{rsvp-attractor}
  An \emph{RSVP attractor} is a non-empty, closed, invariant
  set $\mathcal{O}\subseteq\{(\Phi,\mathbf{v},S)\}$ of the
  field flow such that for every trajectory with bounded
  initial energy, $\mathrm{dist}\bigl((\Phi(t),\mathbf{v}(t),S(t)),\mathcal{O}\bigr)\to0$
  as $t\to\infty$.
  \index{RSVP!attractor|textbf}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Attractor Convergence Theorem}{rsvp-attractor-conv}
  Under the hypotheses of \cref{cor:rsvp-global-exist} and
  bounded entropy production
  ($\int_0^\infty |\dot S|\,dt < \infty$), every admissible
  RSVP trajectory converges to an attractor class
  $\mathcal{O}$ on which $E$ is constant.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By \cref{thm:rsvp-energy-decay}, $E(t)$ is non-increasing
  and bounded below, hence converges to a limit
  $E_\infty \ge 0$. Bounded entropy production implies $S(t)$
  converges (as a non-increasing-variation function bounded
  below), and the Lamphrodyne Stability Theorem
  (\cref{thm:lamphrodyne}) forces
  $\partial S/\partial\Phi \to 0$ along the flow, so the
  driving term for further change in $\Phi$ vanishes in the
  limit. The omega-limit set of any trajectory with these
  properties is therefore a non-empty, closed, invariant set
  on which $E \equiv E_\infty$ --- by definition, an
  attractor $\mathcal{O}$. Standard LaSalle-invariance-type
  argument (applicable here because $E$ is a genuine
  Lyapunov function by \cref{thm:rsvp-energy-decay}) gives
  convergence of the trajectory itself to $\mathcal{O}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{Why this matters}{rsvp-attractor-remark}
  The Attractor Convergence Theorem upgrades RSVP from a
  system of suggestive field equations to a genuine field
  theory with a well-posedness and long-time-behaviour
  package: solutions exist, are unique, depend continuously
  on data, and --- under the physically motivated hypothesis
  of bounded entropy production --- settle into classifiable
  attractor regimes. Chapters~\ref{ch:gravity}--\ref{ch:cosmo-completion}
  may therefore treat gravitational and cosmological
  structures as RSVP attractors without further
  justification of existence.
\end{remark}

\section*{Chapter Summary}
\begin{chapsummary}
  \item Capacity, transport, and constraint are
        independently necessary
        (\cref{thm:three-field-necessity}).
  \item Capacity obeys a continuity equation
        (\cref{thm:capacity-conservation}); constraint
        obeys an accumulation equation
        (\cref{thm:constraint-accumulation-rsvp}).
  \item Reachability volume is proportional to
        $\Phi e^{-S}$ (\cref{thm:rsvp-reachability}) ---
        the central bridge to Part~V.
  \item Lamphrodyne relaxation drives systems toward lower
        constraint (\cref{thm:lamphrodyne}).
  \item Generative admissibility becomes
        $\frac{d}{dt}(\Phi e^{-S}) \ge 0$
        (\cref{thm:rsvp-admissibility}).
  \item RSVP physically realizes the abstract programme of
        Chapters~1--15 (\cref{thm:rsvp-realization}).
  \item The coupled field system is locally well-posed
        (\cref{thm:rsvp-well-posed}) and the energy
        functional $E[\Phi,\mathbf{v},S]$ is non-increasing
        along admissible lamphrodyne flow
        (\cref{thm:rsvp-energy-decay}), giving global
        existence under bounded initial energy
        (\cref{cor:rsvp-global-exist}).
  \item Admissible trajectories with bounded entropy
        production converge to RSVP attractor classes
        (\cref{thm:rsvp-attractor-conv}).
\end{chapsummary}

\subsection*{Exercises}

\begin{exercise}[Physics]
  Show that the lamphrodyne relaxation equation reduces to
  ordinary gradient descent on $S$ when $\Phi$ is treated
  as a positive scalar multiplier. Under what condition
  does relaxation fail to reach a stationary point in
  finite time?
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Using the Capacity Conservation Theorem, derive the
  condition under which a steady state ($\partial\Phi/\partial t = 0$)
  exists with nonzero transport $\mathbf{v}$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Prove that $V_R = K\Phi e^{-S}$ is, up to choice of $K$,
  the unique functional form satisfying monotonicity in
  $\Phi$, anti-monotonicity in $S$, and additivity of $S$
  across independent regions.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Physics]
  Show that a static configuration ($\dot\Phi=\dot S=0$,
  $\mathbf{v}=0$) is always a trivial RSVP attractor in the
  sense of \cref{defn:rsvp-attractor}. Under what condition
  on $\rho_\mathcal{C}$ (\cref{defn:constraint-density}) does
  a non-static attractor exist?
\end{exercise}

% ============================================================
%  CHAPTER 17: GRAVITY AS DISTINCTION DYNAMICS
% ============================================================
\chapter{Gravity as Distinction Dynamics}
\label{ch:gravity}

\begin{epigraph}{%
  Things do not fall because they are pulled.
  They fall because the future is larger in that direction.}%
  {Author}
\end{epigraph}

\begin{objectives}
  \item Derive a gravitational potential from the RSVP
        capacity field.
  \item Prove the Capacity Gradient Theorem and the
        Reachability Gradient Theorem.
  \item Define admissible geodesics and derive the geodesic
        equation from the admissibility-weighted metric.
  \item Prove the Distinction Curvature Theorem and the
        Distinction Poisson Equation.
  \item Prove Gravity as Reachability Optimization, the
        conceptual centrepiece of the chapter.
  \item Derive the Collapse Threshold Theorem and the
        Gravity Realization Theorem.
\end{objectives}

\section{From Capacity to Potential}
\label{sec:ch17-potential}

Chapter~17 does not compete with General Relativity on its
own terms. Within the architecture of this book, gravity
emerges as a consequence of gradients in
distinction-supporting capacity rather than being
introduced as a fundamental force. The conceptual
transition is
\[
  \text{Distinction} \to \text{Capacity} \to \text{Reachability}
  \to \text{Capacity Gradient} \to \text{Gravity}.
\]
The chapter's task is to show that motion toward regions of
higher capacity and lower admissibility loss naturally
produces gravitational-like behaviour.

\begin{definition}{Capacity Potential}{capacity-potential}
  Let $\Phi(x,t)$ be the RSVP capacity field. Define the
  \emph{gravitational potential}
  \[
    \Psi(x,t) = -\log \Phi(x,t).
  \]
  \index{gravity!capacity potential}
\end{definition}

The logarithm appears naturally because reachability
volumes scale multiplicatively (\cref{thm:rsvp-reachability})
while geometric distances scale additively.

\section{Capacity Gradients and Acceleration}
\label{sec:ch17-gradient}

\begin{theorem}{Capacity Gradient Theorem}{capacity-gradient}
  The natural acceleration field generated by capacity
  gradients is
  \[
    \mathbf{g} = -\nabla\Psi.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Motion toward greater future reachability corresponds to
  motion toward larger $\Phi$, by the Reachability Capacity
  Theorem. Since $\Psi = -\log\Phi$, the steepest ascent of
  $\Phi$ equals the steepest descent of $\Psi$. Therefore
  the natural acceleration field is $\mathbf{g} = -\nabla\Psi$.
\end{proof}

This is the RSVP analogue of $\mathbf{g} = -\nabla\phi$ in
Newtonian gravitation.

\begin{theorem}{Reachability Gradient Theorem}{reachability-gradient}
  If $V_R = K\Phi e^{-S}$, then
  \[
    \nabla \log V_R = \nabla\log\Phi - \nabla S.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Taking logarithms, $\log V_R = \log K + \log\Phi - S$.
  Differentiating yields the stated identity.
\end{proof}

This decomposition is important: it shows that
gravity-like behaviour arises from two independent
contributions, \emph{capacity attraction} ($\nabla\log\Phi$)
and \emph{constraint repulsion} ($-\nabla S$).

\section{Admissible Geodesics}
\label{sec:ch17-geodesics}

\begin{definition}{Admissible Geodesic}{admissible-geodesic}
  An \emph{admissible geodesic} satisfies
  \[
    \gamma^* = \operatorname*{arg\,max}_\gamma
    \int_\gamma \Phi e^{-S} \, ds.
  \]
  \index{geodesic!admissible|textbf}
\end{definition}

This is the RSVP replacement for the principle of least
action: trajectories extremize cumulative reachable
capacity rather than a Lagrangian postulated independently.

\begin{theorem}{Admissible Geodesic Theorem}{admissible-geodesic-thm}
  The trajectory maximizing cumulative reachable capacity
  satisfies
  \[
    \frac{d^2 x^i}{dt^2}
    + \Gamma^i_{jk}\frac{dx^j}{dt}\frac{dx^k}{dt} = 0.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  The functional $\int_\gamma \Phi e^{-S}\,ds$ defines a
  metric weighting on state space. Applying Euler--Lagrange
  variation to this functional yields the associated
  geodesic equation with Christoffel symbols
  $\Gamma^i_{jk}$ determined by the induced metric.
\end{proof}

This theorem is the bridge from RSVP to differential
geometry.

\begin{definition}{Distinction Metric}{distinction-metric}
  Define
  \[
    g_{ij} = \Phi e^{-S} \delta_{ij}.
  \]
  \index{distinction metric|textbf}
\end{definition}

This is the simplest admissibility-weighted metric
consistent with \cref{defn:admissible-geodesic}.

\section{Curvature from Capacity}
\label{sec:ch17-curvature}

\begin{theorem}{Distinction Curvature Theorem}{distinction-curvature}
  Spatial variation of $\Phi e^{-S}$ induces nonzero
  curvature.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  If $g_{ij} = \Phi e^{-S}\delta_{ij}$, then derivatives of
  $g_{ij}$ appear in the Christoffel symbols
  $\Gamma^k_{ij}$ (\cref{app:math}). Nonconstant
  $\Phi e^{-S}$ implies nonzero connection coefficients,
  and nonzero connection coefficients imply nonzero
  curvature via the Riemann tensor.
\end{proof}

This provides the RSVP analogue of curved spacetime, with
curvature sourced not by stress-energy directly but by
spatial variation in distinction capacity and constraint.

\section{The Weak-Field Limit}
\label{sec:ch17-weakfield}

\begin{definition}{Distinction Density}{distinction-density-defn}
  Let $\rho_D$ denote the local \emph{distinction density}:
  the rate at which distinctions are produced or destroyed
  per unit volume.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Distinction Poisson Equation}{distinction-poisson}
  In the weak-field regime,
  \[
    \nabla^2 \Psi = \kappa \rho_D.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Assume weak variation $\Phi = \Phi_0 + \delta\Phi$ about
  a background value $\Phi_0$. Linearizing
  $\Psi = -\log\Phi$ gives $\delta\Psi \approx
  -\delta\Phi/\Phi_0$. The Capacity Conservation Theorem
  (\cref{thm:capacity-conservation}) implies that the
  divergence of the capacity flux is proportional to local
  distinction density $\rho_D$. Combining these gives
  $\nabla^2\Psi = \kappa\rho_D$ for an appropriate constant
  $\kappa$.
\end{proof}

This gives the Newtonian limit of RSVP gravity: when
constraint variation is small, the Distinction Poisson
Equation reduces to the ordinary Poisson equation for
gravitational potential.

\begin{definition}{Effective Mass}{effective-mass}
  Define
  \[
    M_D = \int_\Omega \rho_D \, dV.
  \]
\end{definition}

Mass becomes integrated distinction density: a body with
large $M_D$ is a region where distinctions are densely
produced or anchored.

\begin{theorem}{Mass--Capacity Theorem}{mass-capacity}
  Regions of high distinction density generate capacity
  gradients.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By the Distinction Poisson Equation, positive $\rho_D$
  produces nontrivial solutions for $\Psi$. These solutions
  induce gradients in $\Phi$ via $\Psi = -\log\Phi$.
\end{proof}

\section{Gravity as Reachability Optimization}
\label{sec:ch17-optimization}

The next theorem is the conceptual heart of the chapter.

\begin{theorem}{Gravity as Reachability Optimization}{gravity-reachability}
  Gravitational attraction is motion toward regions of
  maximal future reachability.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By $V_R = K\Phi e^{-S}$ (\cref{thm:rsvp-reachability}),
  reachable volume increases with $\Phi$. The acceleration
  field $\mathbf{g} = -\nabla\Psi = \nabla\log\Phi$
  (\cref{thm:capacity-gradient}) points toward increasing
  capacity. Therefore motion under $\mathbf{g}$ follows the
  direction of increasing reachable future volume.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{The conceptual centrepiece}{gravity-remark}
  This is the theorem that makes gravity a consequence of
  admissibility geometry rather than a primitive force.
  Bodies do not attract one another because of an
  irreducible gravitational charge; they move toward one
  another because that motion follows the gradient of
  future possibility.
\end{remark}

\section{Collapse}
\label{sec:ch17-collapse}

\begin{theorem}{Collapse Threshold Theorem}{collapse-threshold}
  If $S$ grows faster than $\log\Phi$, then
  \[
    V_R = K\Phi e^{-S} \to 0.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Immediate from $V_R = K\Phi e^{-S}$: exponential growth
  of $S$ dominates logarithmic growth of $\Phi$, forcing
  $V_R \to 0$.
\end{proof}

This creates a natural route into black-hole-like states:
regions where constraint accumulation outpaces capacity
growth become regions of vanishing reachability, without
requiring an independent postulate of horizon formation.

\section{Curvature--Recoverability Correspondence}
\label{sec:ch17-curv-reco}

The preceding sections established that gravity is a
consequence of capacity gradients. This section sharpens
that claim into a precise correspondence between
distinction-density curvature and the recoverability
machinery of Chapter~\ref{ch:recoverability}, and derives
an analogue of the Raychaudhuri equation governing the
focusing of distinction-bundle trajectories.

\begin{definition}{Distinction-Density Curvature}{dist-density-curvature}
  Let $\rho_D(x)$ be the local distinction density of
  \cref{defn:distinction-density-defn}. Define the
  \emph{distinction-density curvature}
  \[
    \kappa_D(x) = -\Delta\rho_D(x),
  \]
  where $\Delta$ is the Laplace--Beltrami operator on $X$.
  \index{curvature!distinction-density|textbf}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Curvature--Recoverability Correspondence Theorem}%
  {curv-reco-corr}
  $\kappa_D(x) > 0$ at $x$ if and only if $x$ is a local
  maximum of recoverability density: nearby distinctions
  are, on average, more reconstructible at $x$ than at
  neighbouring points.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By the Law of Recoverability (\cref{thm:reco-law}),
  recoverability of a distinction is determined by the
  density of accessible reconstruction pathways in its
  neighbourhood, which by \cref{thm:mass-capacity} is itself
  governed by $\rho_D$. A point $x$ with $\kappa_D(x) =
  -\Delta\rho_D(x) > 0$ is, by the standard interpretation of
  the negative Laplacian, a local maximum of $\rho_D$
  (concave from above), meaning distinction density --- and
  hence reconstruction-pathway density --- is locally
  peaked at $x$ relative to its neighbours. By the
  Reconstruction Theorem (\cref{thm:reconstruction}), peaked
  pathway density corresponds to peaked recoverability.
  Conversely, $\kappa_D(x) \le 0$ corresponds to $x$ being a
  saddle or local minimum of $\rho_D$, where recoverability
  is no higher than at neighbouring points.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}{Geodesic Concentration Theorem}{geodesic-concentration}
  Admissible geodesics (\cref{defn:admissible-geodesic})
  satisfy
  \[
    \frac{d^2 x^i}{d\tau^2} = -\nabla^i \rho_D,
  \]
  to leading order in the weak-field regime: trajectories
  accelerate toward regions of higher distinction density.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By the Distinction Poisson Equation
  (\cref{thm:distinction-poisson}), $\nabla^2\Psi =
  \kappa\rho_D$ in the weak-field regime, so
  $\Psi$ is sourced by $\rho_D$. The Capacity Gradient
  Theorem (\cref{thm:capacity-gradient}) gives
  $\mathbf{g} = -\nabla\Psi$ as the acceleration field along
  admissible geodesics (\cref{thm:admissible-geodesic-thm}).
  Combining, the leading-order acceleration is proportional
  to $-\nabla\Psi$, which by the Poisson relation is sourced
  by $-\nabla^i\rho_D$ up to the constant $\kappa$, absorbed
  here into the parametrisation of $\tau$. This recovers the
  stated geodesic equation.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}{Distinction-Bundle Expansion}{dist-bundle-expansion}
  For a congruence of admissible geodesics with tangent
  field $u^i$, define the \emph{expansion scalar}
  $\theta = \nabla_i u^i$, the \emph{shear} $\sigma$, and the
  \emph{twist} $\omega$, in direct analogy with the
  corresponding quantities for geodesic congruences in
  Riemannian geometry, computed with respect to the
  distinction metric $g_{ij} = \Phi e^{-S}\delta_{ij}$
  (\cref{defn:distinction-metric}).
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Distinction Raychaudhuri Theorem}{dist-raychaudhuri}
  Along a congruence of admissible geodesics,
  \[
    \frac{d\theta}{d\tau}
    = -\frac{1}{3}\theta^2 - \sigma^2 + \omega^2 - \kappa_D.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  The identity is the standard Raychaudhuri equation for a
  geodesic congruence under metric $g_{ij}$
  (\cref{defn:distinction-metric}), with the Ricci-curvature
  source term replaced by $\kappa_D$. By the Distinction
  Curvature Theorem (\cref{thm:distinction-curvature}),
  spatial variation of $\Phi e^{-S}$ induces the curvature of
  $g_{ij}$; by \cref{thm:curv-reco-corr}, this curvature is
  governed at leading order by $\kappa_D$. Substituting
  $\kappa_D$ for the Ricci source term in the standard
  derivation (via the Jacobi equation for geodesic
  deviation, applied to the metric of
  \cref{defn:distinction-metric}) yields the stated
  equation.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}{Recoverability Focusing}{reco-focusing}
  If $\kappa_D \ge 0$ throughout a region and the congruence
  is initially converging ($\theta_0 < 0$) with
  $\sigma^2 \ge \omega^2$, then $\theta \to -\infty$ in
  finite affine parameter: the congruence focuses.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Under the stated hypotheses, every term on the right-hand
  side of \cref{thm:dist-raychaudhuri} is non-positive, so
  $d\theta/d\tau \le -\theta^2/3$. This Riccati-type
  inequality forces $\theta \to -\infty$ in finite $\tau$
  whenever $\theta_0 < 0$, by the standard comparison
  argument for the equation $\dot y = -y^2/3$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{Focusing as collapse}{focusing-collapse-remark}
  Recoverability Focusing is the distinction-geometric
  counterpart of geodesic focusing in general relativity: it
  shows that regions of high distinction density
  ($\kappa_D \ge 0$) actively concentrate admissible
  trajectories, providing the precise mechanism underlying
  the Collapse Threshold Theorem
  (\cref{thm:collapse-threshold}) at the level of individual
  geodesic congruences rather than only at the level of
  aggregate reachability volume.
\end{remark}

\section{Gravity Realized}
\label{sec:ch17-realization}

\begin{theorem}{Gravity Realization Theorem}{gravity-realization}
  Gravity is the geometric manifestation of capacity
  gradients within the admissibility manifold.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By \cref{thm:mass-capacity,thm:distinction-curvature,thm:capacity-gradient,thm:gravity-reachability,thm:curv-reco-corr,thm:geodesic-concentration},
  the chain
  \[
    \rho_D \to \Phi \to g_{ij} \to \Psi \to \mathbf{g}
  \]
  is well defined at each step, and each arrow is
  established by a theorem of this chapter. The composite
  map realizes gravitational attraction as a consequence
  of distinction geometry, and the Distinction Raychaudhuri
  Theorem shows that this geometric attraction focuses
  trajectories in exactly the manner expected of a genuine
  curvature-sourced force.
\end{proof}

At this point Chapter~\ref{ch:expyrotic} can introduce
expyrotic cosmology. The crucial move there is to
interpret cosmic evolution not as expansion of pre-existing
space but as evolution of the global admissibility
manifold $\mathcal{A}(t)$, with cosmological cycles
corresponding to repeated collapse and regeneration of
distinction-producing capacity. That chapter is where RSVP
becomes a cosmology rather than merely a field theory.

\section*{Chapter Summary}
\begin{chapsummary}
  \item The gravitational potential is
        $\Psi = -\log\Phi$ (\cref{defn:capacity-potential}).
  \item Acceleration follows the capacity gradient:
        $\mathbf{g} = -\nabla\Psi$
        (\cref{thm:capacity-gradient}).
  \item Admissible geodesics extremize cumulative reachable
        capacity and satisfy the standard geodesic equation
        under the distinction metric $g_{ij} = \Phi e^{-S}\delta_{ij}$
        (\cref{thm:admissible-geodesic-thm}).
  \item Spatial variation in capacity and constraint
        induces curvature (\cref{thm:distinction-curvature}).
  \item In the weak-field limit, RSVP gravity reduces to
        the ordinary Poisson equation
        (\cref{thm:distinction-poisson}).
  \item Gravitational attraction is motion toward maximal
        future reachability
        (\cref{thm:gravity-reachability}) ---
        the conceptual centrepiece of the chapter.
  \item Runaway constraint growth produces collapse
        (\cref{thm:collapse-threshold}).
  \item Distinction-density curvature $\kappa_D$
        corresponds exactly to local peaks of recoverability
        density (\cref{thm:curv-reco-corr}), and sources
        geodesic acceleration directly
        (\cref{thm:geodesic-concentration}).
  \item Geodesic congruences obey a distinction-theoretic
        Raychaudhuri equation
        (\cref{thm:dist-raychaudhuri}), giving a precise
        focusing mechanism underlying gravitational collapse
        (\cref{cor:reco-focusing}).
\end{chapsummary}

\subsection*{Exercises}

\begin{exercise}[Physics]
  Verify that the Distinction Poisson Equation reduces
  exactly to the Newtonian Poisson equation
  $\nabla^2\phi = 4\pi G\rho$ under an appropriate
  identification of $\kappa$ and $\rho_D$.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Using the Collapse Threshold Theorem, characterize the
  boundary in $(\Phi, S)$-space separating collapsing from
  non-collapsing regions.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Show that the admissible geodesic equation reduces to a
  straight line when $\Phi e^{-S}$ is constant throughout
  the domain. Interpret this physically.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Physics]
  Using \cref{cor:reco-focusing}, estimate the affine
  parameter at which focusing occurs given initial expansion
  $\theta_0 < 0$ and $\sigma=\omega=0$. Compare to the
  analogous estimate in the standard Raychaudhuri equation
  of general relativity.
\end{exercise}

% ============================================================
%  CHAPTER 18: EXPYROTIC COSMOLOGY
% ============================================================
\chapter{Expyrotic Cosmology}
\label{ch:expyrotic}

\begin{epigraph}{%
  The question is no longer how large the universe is.
  The question is how much future remains reachable.}%
  {Author}
\end{epigraph}

\begin{objectives}
  \item Define cosmological admissible volume and
        cosmological constraint.
  \item Prove the Cosmological Reachability Theorem.
  \item Prove the Admissibility Collapse Theorem and define
        the admissibility singularity.
  \item Prove the Expyrotic Necessity Theorem and the
        Expyrotic Cycle Theorem.
  \item Derive the Cosmological Evolution Equation and the
        Apparent Expansion Theorem.
  \item Prove the Expyrotic Admissibility Theorem.
\end{objectives}

\section{Reachability Replaces Size}
\label{sec:ch18-replaces}

Chapter~18 is where the framework stops looking like a
field theory and becomes a cosmology. The central idea is
not ordinary expansion. The preceding chapters have already
defined the primary quantity governing a system's future:
admissible volume. The question ``how large is the
universe?'' is replaced by the question ``how much future
remains reachable?'' In this formulation, cosmology becomes
the dynamics of admissible volume.

\begin{definition}{Cosmological Admissible Volume}{cosmo-adm}
  Define
  \[
    \mathcal{V}(t) = \Vol\bigl(\mathcal{A}(t)\bigr).
  \]
  \index{cosmology!admissible volume|textbf}
\end{definition}

Unlike standard cosmology, the scale factor $a(t)$ is no
longer fundamental. Instead $\mathcal{V}(t)$ is fundamental.

\begin{theorem}{Cosmological Reachability Theorem}{cosmo-reach}
  The long-term future of a universe is determined by
  $\mathcal{V}(t)$ rather than by physical size.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Physical volume measures spatial extent. Reachability
  volume measures future possibility. A large universe may
  possess $\mathcal{V} \approx 0$ if all trajectories
  terminate. A small universe may possess large
  $\mathcal{V}$ if many futures remain reachable. Therefore
  future structure depends on $\mathcal{V}$ rather than on
  spatial size.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{From geometry to possibility}{cosmo-philosophy}
  This theorem is philosophically significant: it shifts
  cosmology from a science of geometry to a science of
  possibility.
\end{remark}

\section{Cosmological Constraint and Collapse}
\label{sec:ch18-collapse}

\begin{definition}{Cosmological Constraint Functional}{cosmo-constraint}
  \[
    \mathcal{S}(t) = \int_U S(x,t)\, dV.
  \]
\end{definition}

This is the universe-wide RSVP entropy field, integrated
over the entire spatial domain $U$.

\begin{theorem}{Admissibility Collapse Theorem}{adm-collapse}
  If $\mathcal{S}(t) \to \infty$ while $\Phi$ remains
  bounded, then $\mathcal{V}(t) \to 0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By Chapter~16, $V_R = K\Phi e^{-S}$. Integrating over the
  universe gives
  \[
    \mathcal{V} = K \int_U \Phi e^{-S} \, dV.
  \]
  As $S \to \infty$ pointwise with $\Phi$ bounded, the
  exponential factor vanishes throughout $U$. Therefore
  $\mathcal{V} \to 0$.
\end{proof}

This is the RSVP analogue of heat death.

\begin{definition}{Admissibility Singularity}{adm-singularity}
  An \emph{admissibility singularity} occurs when
  $\mathcal{V}(t) = 0$.
  \index{singularity!admissibility|textbf}
\end{definition}

Notice that no divergence of density and no divergence of
curvature is required for this singularity. It is defined
purely by exhaustion of futures.

\section{Expyrotic Necessity}
\label{sec:ch18-necessity}

\begin{theorem}{Expyrotic Necessity Theorem}{expyrotic-necessity}
  A universe satisfying $\mathcal{V}(t) \to 0$ must either
  terminate or undergo distinction renewal.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  When $\mathcal{V} = 0$, no future trajectories remain, and
  continuation is impossible. To avoid termination, new
  reachable states must be generated, which requires
  creation of new distinction capacity. Therefore renewal
  is necessary if termination is to be avoided.
\end{proof}

This theorem is essentially the Principle of Regeneration
(Chapter~\ref{ch:regeneration}) applied
cosmologically.

\begin{definition}{Cosmological Renewal Operator}{cosmo-renewal}
  \[
    \mathfrak{E} : \mathcal{A}_{\min} \to \mathcal{A}_{\max}.
  \]
  \index{expyrotic renewal operator|textbf}
\end{definition}

The expyrotic operator $\mathfrak{E}$ maps near-zero
admissibility volume to renewed admissibility volume.

\begin{theorem}{Expyrotic Cycle Theorem}{expyrotic-cycle}
  A cosmological cycle satisfies
  \[
    \mathcal{V}_{\max} \to \mathcal{V}_{\min}
    \xrightarrow{\ \mathfrak{E}\ } \mathcal{V}_{\max}.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Entropy accumulation reduces admissible volume. By the
  Admissibility Collapse Theorem, $\mathcal{V}$ approaches
  zero. The renewal operator $\mathfrak{E}$ creates new
  distinction capacity, by definition. Therefore
  $\mathcal{V}$ returns to a high value after renewal.
\end{proof}

\section{Cosmological Evolution}
\label{sec:ch18-evolution}

\begin{definition}{Cosmological Potential}{cosmo-potential}
  \[
    \mathcal{U}(\mathcal{V}) = \lambda \mathcal{S} - \mu \Phi.
  \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}{Cosmological Evolution Equation}{cosmo-evolution}
  The admissibility volume obeys
  \[
    \frac{d\mathcal{V}}{dt} = \alpha \Phi - \beta \mathcal{S}.
  \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Capacity production increases reachable futures at rate
  proportional to $\Phi$; constraint accumulation decreases
  reachable futures at rate proportional to $\mathcal{S}$.
  Combining both contributions linearly yields
  $\frac{d\mathcal{V}}{dt} = \alpha\Phi - \beta\mathcal{S}$.
\end{proof}

This is the simplest cosmological RSVP equation consistent
with the Capacity Conservation and Constraint Accumulation
Theorems of Chapter~16.

\begin{theorem}{Apparent Expansion Theorem}{apparent-expansion}
  Growth of $\mathcal{V}(t)$ can be observed as spatial
  expansion even when the primary dynamics occur in
  admissibility space.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Increasing admissible volume enlarges the set of
  reachable configurations. Observers embedded within the
  manifold interpret this enlargement as increasing spatial
  separation between distinguishable configurations, since
  spatial separation is itself a derived distinction.
  Hence admissibility growth may project as cosmological
  expansion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{Expansion as projection}{expansion-projection}
  This is where RSVP diverges from standard cosmology.
  Expansion becomes projection: what is observed as the
  metric expansion of space is, in this framework, the
  shadow cast by growth in admissible volume.
\end{remark}

\section{Cosmological Memory}
\label{sec:ch18-memory}

\begin{theorem}{Cosmological Memory Theorem}{cosmo-memory}
  Successive expyrotic cycles need not be independent.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  If $\reco(\mathcal{U}) > 0$, then information from
  previous cycles survives renewal. By the Law of
  Recoverability (Chapter~\ref{ch:recoverability}), partial
  reconstruction remains possible across the renewal
  operator $\mathfrak{E}$. Therefore cosmological memory may
  persist.
\end{proof}

This is the direct cosmological analogue of repair, and it
sets up Chapter~19's treatment of cross-cycle conservation.

\section{Expyrotic Admissibility}
\label{sec:ch18-admissibility}

\begin{theorem}{Expyrotic Admissibility Theorem}{expyrotic-adm}
  Cosmological renewal is generatively admissible.
  \parencite{steinhardt2002,penrose2010}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Renewal transforms $\mathcal{V}_{\min}$ into
  $\mathcal{V}_{\max}$, so $\Delta\mathcal{V} > 0$. By the
  Generative Admissibility Principle
  (\cref{prc:gap-final}), $\frac{d}{dt}\Vol(\mathcal{A}) \ge 0$
  defines generative admissibility. Hence cosmological
  renewal satisfies the defining condition of generative
  admissibility.
\end{proof}

This theorem is the reason Chapter~15 already cited
expyrotic renewal as a corollary of the Generative
Admissibility Principle. It closes the loop between
cosmology and the rest of the book: the universe itself
becomes the largest regenerative system, cycling between
admissibility exhaustion and admissibility renewal. In the
architecture of this monograph, the Big Bang is no longer
the beginning of existence but one instance of a more
general repair operation acting on the global distinction
manifold.

\section{The Reintegration Operator}
\label{sec:ch18-reintegration}

The Expyrotic Cycle Theorem (\cref{thm:expyrotic-cycle})
asserts the existence of a renewal operator
$\mathfrak{E}:\mathcal{A}_{\min}\to\mathcal{A}_{\max}$
without specifying its mechanism. This section supplies a
concrete construction --- a reintegration operator acting
on cosmic microwave background structure --- and proves that
it possesses a fixed point characterising the
post-renewal state, together with a theorem on the
asymptotic suppression of large-scale entropy gradients.

\begin{definition}{Reintegration Kernel}{reintegration-kernel}
  Let $\Phi_\mathrm{CMB}(x,t)$ denote the capacity field
  restricted to the cosmic microwave background surface, and
  let $K:\mathbb{R}_{\ge0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\ge0}$ be an
  integrable kernel supported on $[0,T_P]$ for renewal
  period $T_P$. The \emph{reintegration operator} is
  \[
    (\mathcal{K}\Phi)(x,t)
    = \int_{t-T_P}^{t} K(t-t')\, \Phi_\mathrm{CMB}(x,t')\, dt'.
  \]
  \index{reintegration operator|textbf}
\end{definition}

The reintegration operator aggregates capacity-field
structure from the preceding cycle, weighted by recency
through $K$, to construct the capacity field with which the
next cycle begins.

\begin{theorem}{Reintegration Fixed-Point Theorem}{reintegration-fixed-point}
  Suppose $\mathcal{K}$ acts on a compact, convex set of
  admissible capacity-field configurations
  $\mathcal{C} \subset \{\Phi_\mathrm{CMB}\}$ and is
  continuous on $\mathcal{C}$. Then there exists
  $\Phi^*\in\mathcal{C}$ with $\Phi^* = \mathcal{K}(\Phi^*)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By construction, $\mathcal{K}$ is a bounded linear
  (hence continuous) operator on the space of capacity-field
  configurations, since it is given by convolution against
  the integrable kernel $K$. Under the stated compactness
  hypothesis on $\mathcal{C}$ and convexity, the
  Schauder Fixed-Point Theorem applies directly to
  $\mathcal{K}|_\mathcal{C} : \mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}$
  (well-defined since $\mathcal{K}$ preserves admissibility
  by the non-negativity of $K$ and $\Phi_\mathrm{CMB}$),
  giving existence of $\Phi^*\in\mathcal{C}$ with
  $\Phi^* = \mathcal{K}(\Phi^*)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{Interpretation of the fixed point}{reintegration-interpretation}
  The fixed point $\Phi^*$ represents a self-consistent
  post-renewal capacity configuration: a capacity field
  that, when reintegrated through $\mathcal{K}$ across one
  renewal period, reproduces itself. This is the
  mathematical content of the Cosmological Memory Theorem
  (\cref{thm:cosmo-memory}): it identifies the specific
  configurations through which cross-cycle correlation
  $\reco(\mathcal{U})>0$ can be realized, rather than merely
  asserting their possible existence.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}{Asymptotic Entropy-Gradient Suppression Theorem}%
  {entropy-gradient-suppression}
  Under reintegration via a fixed point $\Phi^*$ of
  \cref{thm:reintegration-fixed-point}, large-scale entropy
  gradients are asymptotically suppressed:
  \[
    \lim_{t\to\infty} \nabla S(x,t) = 0
  \]
  uniformly on length scales exceeding the reintegration
  correlation length $\ell_K$ determined by the support of
  $K$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By the Constraint Accumulation Theorem
  (\cref{thm:constraint-accumulation-rsvp}),
  $\partial S/\partial t = \alpha D - \beta R + \gamma\nabla^2 S$.
  At the fixed point $\Phi^*$, reintegration injects capacity
  uniformly on scales up to $\ell_K$ (by the averaging
  property of convolution against $K$), which by the
  Lamphrodyne Stability Theorem (\cref{thm:lamphrodyne})
  drives $\partial S/\partial\Phi \to 0$ on those scales as
  $t\to\infty$, since the system relaxes toward the fixed
  point along trajectories satisfying
  $dS/dt = -\mu(\partial S/\partial\Phi)^2 \le 0$ with
  equality only when $\partial S/\partial\Phi = 0$. Spatial
  uniformity of the injected capacity on scales $\ge\ell_K$
  forces $S$ itself to become spatially uniform on those
  scales in the limit, hence $\nabla S \to 0$ uniformly
  there.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}{Horizon smoothing}{horizon-smoothing-remark}
  The Asymptotic Entropy-Gradient Suppression Theorem
  supplies the mathematical mechanism behind horizon
  smoothing in the expyrotic picture: reintegration through
  $\mathcal{K}$ does not merely renew admissible volume in
  aggregate (\cref{thm:expyrotic-adm}) but actively erases
  large-scale entropy gradients left over from the
  preceding cycle, in direct analogy with the role played by
  inflation in suppressing large-scale inhomogeneity in the
  standard cosmological picture --- but derived here from
  the reintegration operator rather than postulated as an
  independent inflationary phase.
\end{remark}

\section*{Chapter Summary}
\begin{chapsummary}
  \item Cosmology is governed by admissible volume
        $\mathcal{V}(t) = \Vol(\mathcal{A}(t))$, not by
        spatial size (\cref{thm:cosmo-reach}).
  \item Unbounded constraint growth drives
        $\mathcal{V} \to 0$, the admissibility singularity
        (\cref{thm:adm-collapse,defn:adm-singularity}).
  \item Approach to $\mathcal{V} = 0$ forces either
        termination or renewal
        (\cref{thm:expyrotic-necessity}).
  \item Cosmological cycles alternate between
        $\mathcal{V}_{\max}$ and $\mathcal{V}_{\min}$ via
        the renewal operator $\mathfrak{E}$
        (\cref{thm:expyrotic-cycle}).
  \item Observed expansion may be the projection of growth
        in admissible volume
        (\cref{thm:apparent-expansion}).
  \item Cosmological renewal is generatively admissible
        (\cref{thm:expyrotic-adm}), making expyrotic
        cosmology the cosmological instance of the
        Generative Admissibility Principle.
  \item The renewal operator $\mathfrak{E}$ is realized
        concretely by a reintegration operator $\mathcal{K}$
        possessing a fixed point under standard compactness
        hypotheses (\cref{thm:reintegration-fixed-point}).
  \item Reintegration at the fixed point asymptotically
        suppresses large-scale entropy gradients
        (\cref{thm:entropy-gradient-suppression}), giving a
        derived mechanism for horizon smoothing.
\end{chapsummary}

\subsection*{Exercises}

\begin{exercise}
  Using the Cosmological Evolution Equation, determine the
  condition on $\alpha, \beta, \Phi, \mathcal{S}$ under
  which a steady-state universe ($d\mathcal{V}/dt = 0$) is
  possible without renewal.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Physics]
  Compare the Admissibility Singularity
  (\cref{defn:adm-singularity}) to the standard Big Crunch.
  What observational signature, if any, would distinguish
  the two?
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Prove that $\reco(\mathcal{U}) = 0$ is sufficient (though
  not necessary) for successive expyrotic cycles to be
  statistically independent.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[Physics]
  Show that if $K$ is taken to be a Dirac delta at
  $t'=t-T_P$ (perfect periodicity, no averaging), the
  Reintegration Fixed-Point Theorem still applies but the
  Entropy-Gradient Suppression Theorem fails. What does this
  imply about the necessity of kernel smoothing for horizon
  smoothing?
\end{exercise}

% ============================================================
%  CHAPTER 19: COSMOLOGICAL COMPLETION
% ============================================================
\chapter{Cosmological Completion}
\label{ch:cosmo-completion}

\begin{epigraph}{%
  Nothing essential is destroyed. It is only made
  unreachable.}%
  {Author}
\end{epigraph}

\begin{objectives}
  \item Define the global cosmological capacity functional
        and prove its conservation across cycles.
  \item Define the distinction horizon and prove the
        Distinction Horizon Theorem.
  \item Prove the Cycle Memory Theorem and the Universe as
        Repair System Theorem.
  \item Prove the No Terminal Equilibrium Theorem.
  \item Prove the Cosmological Productivity and
        Cosmological Optimality Theorems.
  \item Prove the Cosmological Completion Theorem, closing
        Part~VI.
\end{objectives}

\section{What Is Conserved}
\label{sec:ch19-conserved}

Chapter~19 introduces no fundamentally new machinery. Its
role is to complete the cosmology of Chapter~18 and connect
the physical realization back to the rest of the monograph.
Chapters~16--18 introduced the RSVP triple
$(\Phi,\mathbf{v},S)$, gravity as admissibility geometry,
and expyrotic renewal. The remaining question is: what
global quantity is actually conserved across cycles? The
answer is not matter, not energy, and not information in
the Shannon sense. The natural conserved object in this
framework is distinction-producing capacity.

\begin{definition}{Cosmological Distinction Capacity}{cosmo-distinction-capacity}
  Define
  \[
    \mathcal{C}(t) = \int_U \Phi(x,t)\, e^{-S(x,t)} \, dV.
  \]
\end{definition}

This is the global admissible capacity of the universe. It
is closely related to $\mathcal{V}(t)$ but distinct: $\mathcal{V}$
measures actual admissible volume, while $\mathcal{C}$
measures the capacity to generate admissible volume.

\section{Cosmological Capacity Conservation}
\label{sec:ch19-conservation}

\begin{theorem}{Cosmological Capacity Conservation}{cosmo-capacity}
